    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.21, F32.0, F33.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I... I came in because... ever since my grandson was killed... things just haven't been right. My daughter, she's been coming over, and she'll sit right beside me on the sofa and just hold my hand. She has such delicate hands, you know. She used to play the piano so beautifully when she was a girl. We had this lovely mahogany upright—or was it oak? I think it was oak. I always told her she could have been a concert pianist if she’d just kept at it instead of going off to study business at the university. But, oh, what was I saying? \n\nRight, so... she sits there holding my hand, and I know I love her. I know I do. But... I cannot feel the warmth of it inside me at all. Not anymore. It's just... nothing. I don't know why. \n\nDid I mention I might have left my porch light on? It looked like rain when she drove me here, and I hate coming back to a dark house...\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice you couldn't feel the warmth of your daughter's hand?\n\nPatient: Let me think... it's been exactly six days. Yes, six days since... since my grandson was killed. It happened right outside the community center, you know. \n\nI used to go to that very community center every Wednesday morning for the bingo games. Helen Miller runs them, and she always brings those little powdered sugar cookies from the bakery on Main Street. I asked her to pick some up for me once, but she said they only sell them by the dozen and she didn't want to carry the large box. Can you imagine? It’s just a cardboard box. But... yes, it was right outside those front doors where it happened. Six days ago. \n\nAnd ever since that very day, I just... I look at my daughter's hand on mine, and I feel absolutely nothing. She had her nails painted a lovely shade of peach recently, though. The salon she goes to does such a nice job with cuticles. I should really ask her for their number, though I don't get out much these days. Do you think peach is too young a color for someone my age?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Oh, well... I'm not really sure, honestly. I suppose I haven't been paying much attention to meals or the clock. I did make a pot of chicken noodle soup the other day. It was the recipe my mother used to make when I was a little girl. She would always put in extra celery, the really leafy parts, because she said that's where all the flavor was. We used to grow celery in our backyard garden, right next to the tomatoes. Those tomatoes were so big and red, you could just slice them up and eat them like an apple with a little dash of salt. Have you ever tried that? It's wonderful in the summer. \n\nBut... what were you asking? Oh, appetite. I don't really know if it's changed. I just... I haven't really noticed. I think I left the soup on the stove too long anyway, the noodles got all mushy. I hate mushy noodles, don't you?\n\nDoctor: Do you find pleasure in things you used to enjoy before your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? Oh... I don't know. Like I was saying before about my daughter holding my hand... I know I love her, but I just can't feel the warmth of it inside me anymore. Nothing feels good or... or warm. \n\nI used to really enjoy my garden. I had these beautiful hydrangeas out front. They were the blue kind, you know, because of the soil acidity. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he always tried to grow them but his always turned out pink. He was so stubborn, he wouldn't listen when I told him to add coffee grounds to the dirt. He used to drink that instant coffee anyway, which I always thought was just dreadful. How can anyone drink that powdery stuff? Real coffee needs to be percolated. My husband, God rest his soul, bought me a beautiful silver percolator for our tenth anniversary. Or was it our fifteenth? It had this little glass knob on top where you could watch the water bubbling up. \n\nBut... no, I don't think I feel much pleasure in anything right now. It's just all sort of blank. Do you think Mr. Henderson is still watering my hydrangeas while I'm here? I left him a spare key, but he's getting so forgetful these days.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there's anything you could have done to prevent your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Prevent it? I... I really don't know. I wasn't even there, you see. He had just left my house a little while before. I had ironed his shirt for him that morning. It was a very handsome blue button-down, a cotton blend. You have to be so careful with those cotton blends, though. If the iron is even a little bit too hot, it just scorches the fabric right away and leaves that awful shiny mark on the collar. \n\nMy mother used to have one of those heavy cast-iron presses, the kind you actually had to heat up on the wood stove. Can you imagine doing that today? She would spend all of Monday morning just pressing my father's work trousers. He worked at the bank downtown, the one on the corner of Elm and 4th that closed down back in the eighties. They had the most beautiful marble floors in that lobby. They kept them so polished you could practically see your reflection, and you could hear your shoes clicking so loudly when you walked in to make a deposit. They used to give out those little paper lollipops to the children, the ones with the safety loop handle. \n\nBut... oh, what were we talking about? Did I answer your question? I just... I don't know if I could have stopped it. I really don't. Everything from that day is just a blur.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty? I... I don't know if guilt is the right word. I just feel so empty about it all. Like I said, I wasn't there. I was at home, probably cleaning up the kitchen. I always wipe down the counters after breakfast. I use that lemon-scented cleaner, the one in the yellow bottle. My daughter hates the smell of it, she says it reminds her of a hospital, but I've always loved citrus. We had a lemon tree when I was a girl, out in California. My father planted it when they first moved out there. \n\nCalifornia was so different back then. The air smelled like orange blossoms and the ocean. We used to drive down the coast on Sundays in his old Ford. It was this beautiful deep green color, with those big whitewall tires. He kept it so clean, he would wash it every Saturday afternoon with a bucket of soapy water and a big sponge. He'd let me help sometimes, but I usually just ended up getting more water on myself than the car. \n\nBut... what did you ask me? Guilt? I... I'm not sure. I don't really feel much of anything. It's just... it's all so far away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: Sad? I... well, I wouldn't say I feel sad exactly. It's more like I just don't feel much of anything. It's like being wrapped in a thick wool blanket, you know? The kind that's so heavy you can barely move under it. \n\nI actually have a blanket just like that at home. It's a Pendleton, a beautiful plaid pattern with deep reds and forest greens. My husband and I bought it on a trip to Oregon, oh, it must have been forty years ago now. We drove all the way up the coast from California. The Redwoods were just magnificent. We stayed in this little cabin near Eureka, and the fog would roll in so thick off the ocean every morning you couldn't see the car parked right outside the window. We had breakfast at this tiny diner where the waitress called everyone \"hon\" and they served the biggest blueberry pancakes I've ever seen. The syrup came in these little glass pitchers with a metal thumb-flap on the lid. I always try to find those pitchers for my own kitchen, but the new ones just feel so flimsy, don't they? Everything is made of plastic now. \n\nBut... oh dear, I'm sorry. Sadness. No, I don't think I'm sad. I just... I don't feel much at all. Just that heavy, blank feeling.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt this emptiness and lack of feeling inside?\n\nPatient: How long? Well, let me think... it's been exactly six days. Ever since he was killed right outside the community center. \n\nYou know, they just painted the front doors of that community center a bright cherry red last spring. I always thought it was a bit loud for such an old brick building, but the mayor's wife insisted on it. She was in charge of the local beautification committee. I used to be on a committee just like that, back when we lived in Pasadena in the sixties. We helped organize one of the smaller floats for the Rose Parade. We used thousands of real marigolds one year, and the smell was just so strong, very earthy and sharp. You had to glue them on one by one by one. My fingers were sticky with floral glue for a week, and I somehow got it all over my wedding ring. My husband had to take it to the jeweler on Colorado Boulevard to have it professionally cleaned. The jeweler was this lovely older gentleman who always wore a silk bow tie, even in the dead of summer... \n\nOh dear, I'm rambling again, aren't I? I'm so sorry. My mind just wanders off. What did you ask me? How many days? Yes, six days. It's just been six days of this... this nothingness.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy before your grandson's death that you still find some pleasure in now?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? I... no. No, I don't think so. I actually came in today because... well, my daughter was here earlier. She can sit right beside me, holding my hand, and I know in my head that I love her, but I just... I cannot feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It's just flat. \n\nShe has such beautiful hands, my daughter. Long, elegant fingers, just like her grandmother. My mother used to play the piano, you know. A beautiful upright Baldwin we had in the parlor. She would play Chopin on Sunday afternoons while my father read the paper. The Sunday paper was enormous back then, it seemed to take up the whole dining room table. He always read the business section first, and he'd smoke his pipe. It was a cherrywood pipe, and the tobacco smelled so sweet, like vanilla and roasted nuts. He kept it in a special humidor on his desk, a heavy brass box with a little dial on the front. I used to love spinning that dial when he wasn't looking. The desk was mahogany, very solid, with clawed feet that looked like eagles holding glass balls... \n\nI'm sorry, I'm doing it again. You asked about things I enjoy. No, I don't feel pleasure from anything right now. Even when I should, like with my daughter... there's just no feeling there.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that still bring you a little bit of joy or happiness?\n\nPatient: Joy? No, doctor. I... I really don't think so. I know I just said this, but it's really why I'm here. It's my daughter. She was just here, sitting right next to me, holding my hand so tightly. And I look at her, and my brain tells me, \"This is your daughter, you love her,\" but... there's just no warmth. None at all. It's like the feeling is broken. \n\nShe was wearing a lovely yellow sweater today, though. A very soft cashmere. I bought her one just like it when she went off to college, oh, it must have been 1978. Or was it '79? She went to school in Boston, and the winters there are just dreadful. I remember visiting her in February once, and the snow was piled up higher than the cars on the street. We tried to walk to this little Italian bakery she liked in the North End, but the wind was blowing right off the harbor, cutting right through my coat. My coat was a heavy wool pea coat, navy blue with those big anchor buttons. You don't see buttons like that anymore, they were real brass, heavy and cold to the touch. The bakery smelled like anise and toasted almonds, and they had these little cookies shaped like pine nuts...\n\nBut no, I'm sorry. Joy. No, I don't feel any joy or happiness, not even with my own daughter holding my hand. Just... nothing.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or things that you think should bring you happiness, but don't?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, that's exactly it. That's why I'm sitting here talking to you. It's like I've been trying to explain... my daughter, she was just here. She was holding my hand, and I know I love her. I should be feeling that love, that happiness of having her close by, especially now, after... after my grandson was killed. But there's just... I cannot feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It's completely gone.\n\nWe used to hold hands all the time when she was a little girl. We'd walk down to the corner store—Mr. Hooper's store, it was called. He always had those big glass jars of penny candy lined up on the counter. The root beer barrels were her favorite, but I always preferred the little strawberry candies with the soft centers. They came in wrappers that looked like real strawberries. You had to be careful, though, because if they sat in the jar too long during the summer, they'd all melt together into one giant, sticky lump. Mr. Hooper had this big ceiling fan that just slowly spun around, tick-tick-tick, trying to keep the place cool, but it never really worked. He had a calico cat that slept on top of the ice cream freezer... \n\nOh, goodness, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I'm so sorry, doctor. My mind is just a sieve lately. But yes, to answer your question... things that should make me happy, like my daughter being here... I just can't feel it. There's no warmth at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have moments where you feel something, anything, even if it's just for a brief instant, since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I know I must sound like a broken record to you, doctor, I really do. I feel like I'm just saying the exact same thing over and over. But no, not even for a second. That is the entire reason I came in today. Ever since my grandson was killed... my daughter can sit right beside me, just like she was doing earlier, holding my hand, and I know I love her, but I just cannot feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It's just empty. \n\nWhen she was holding my hand today, I was looking at her wedding band. It's a very simple gold band, very traditional. My own wedding band is gold, too, 18 karat. We bought them at a little jeweler downtown, Mr. Peterson's shop. He had this little jeweler's loupe, but he also had a real monocle he wore sometimes. You never see people wearing monocles anymore, do you? It was attached to a fine black ribbon. He had a pocket watch, too, on a long gold chain that draped across his waistcoat. His vest was a dark green velvet, with tiny little pearl buttons all down the front. The shop always smelled like brass polish and old paper, and he kept mints in a little crystal dish by the register... \n\nBut no. To answer you, no. Not even for a brief instant. Just... nothing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are to blame in any way for your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Blame? No, no, I don't think so. I mean, how could I be? I wasn't even there. I was at home, sitting in my favorite armchair. It's a floral print, very comfortable, with those rolled arms. I bought it at a furniture store that used to be on Main Street... oh, what was it called? Sullivan's. Yes, Sullivan's Furniture. Mr. Sullivan was a very tall man, always wore a bowtie. He had this booming voice that would just echo through the showroom. The showroom had these huge plate glass windows, and in the winter, the condensation would freeze on the inside, making these beautiful, intricate frost patterns. They looked like little ferns and stars, delicate little ice crystals. I used to trace them with my finger when I was a girl, waiting for my mother to finish looking at the dining sets. She loved mahogany, always said it was the only wood fit for a proper dining room... \n\nBut, oh, I'm sorry. No, I don't feel like I'm to blame. I just... I don't feel much of anything at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, I... I don't think so. I mean, like I said, I wasn't there when it happened, so there wasn't anything I could have done differently. I was just at home. I was actually watching that old game show on television, the one with the spinning wheel. Pat Sajak, I think his name is? Though I always preferred the older hosts, from when television was still in black and white. We got our first television set in 1956. It was a Zenith, a big heavy wooden cabinet model. My husband, rest his soul, he had to get the neighbor, Mr. Henderson, to help him carry it up the front steps. Mr. Henderson was a stout man, always smoked a pipe. Cherry tobacco, it smelled like. He worked down at the railyard, and he'd come home with soot on his collar. His wife, Martha, she was famous in the neighborhood for her peach cobbler. She'd bring one over every time somebody moved in or had a baby. She used fresh peaches from a tree in her own backyard... \n\nOh, dear, I've lost the thread again, haven't I? I'm so sorry. But no, to answer you... I don't feel guilty. I just feel so... empty.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have moments where you feel like your emotional numbness lifts, even if it's just for a very short time?\n\nPatient: I know we just talked about this, doctor, I really do feel like I'm repeating myself. But no, it never lifts. Like I told you earlier, that's exactly why I made this appointment in the first place. Ever since my grandson was killed, my daughter can sit right beside me, holding my hand, and I know I love her, I really do, but I just cannot feel the warmth of it inside me at all. Nothing. \n\nHer hands were a bit chilly today, actually. She's always had poor circulation, just like her father. My husband used to wear these thick wool socks around the house, even in the dead of summer. Gray ones, with a little red stripe at the top. We used to order them from a catalog that came in the mail. Our mail carrier back then was a lovely man named Arthur. He had a terrible limp, poor thing, from the war, but he never complained. He carried this massive leather satchel, real thick cowhide. It had a brass buckle that was always polished so bright you could see your reflection in it. I had a handbag with a buckle like that once, bought it on a trip to Chicago in... oh, it must have been 1968. We took the train, the Pullman cars, and the dining car had these pristine white tablecloths and heavy silver cutlery. They don't make silverware like that anymore, it all feels so flimsy now... \n\nOh, dear. I'm doing it again, aren't I? I am so sorry. But no, to answer your question, it never lifts. It's just that same emptiness all the time.\n\nDoctor: Has the emptiness you've been feeling changed in intensity at all since it started six days ago?\n\nPatient: Changed? No, I don't... I don't think so. It's been exactly the same since Tuesday. Six days now, just... just this flat, hollow feeling. Tuesday was always grocery day for me and my sister, Eleanor. We'd go to the A&P down on 4th Street. They had the best produce section, always kept the apples so shiny. Eleanor loved the Granny Smiths, said they were the only ones tart enough for a proper pie. She baked a pie for the church bake sale every single year, right up until her arthritis got too bad. Her hands would just swell up terribly, poor thing. She tried wearing those copper bracelets, you know, the ones they used to advertise in the back of magazines? I never believed they worked, but she swore by them. She ordered hers from a catalog, I think it was... oh, maybe Sears Roebuck? They used to sell everything in those catalogs, even entire houses. Kit houses, they called them. You'd order it, and all the lumber and nails would just arrive on a train... \n\nI'm sorry, doctor. I'm drifting again. But no, the emptiness hasn't changed at all. It's just constant.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Cry? Oh, I... I don't know. Maybe I feel like I should be crying, but nothing happens. Like I was telling you before, it's just this awful blankness. That's really why I'm sitting here talking to you today. Ever since my grandson passed, my daughter will come over and just hold my hand, and I know in my head that I love her so much, but I just can't feel any of that warmth inside. It's just... nothing. 

She held my hand just like that when she was a little girl, crossing the street. We lived on Elm Street then, and the traffic could be so dreadful, especially when the factory let out at 5 o'clock. The whistle would blow, you could hear it all over town, and then all those cars would just come pouring down the hill. Mostly Fords and Chevrolets back then, big heavy things with those beautiful chrome bumpers. My husband drove a Bel Air, a two-tone blue and white. He kept it so clean you could eat off the hood. Every Sunday afternoon, he'd be out there with a bucket and a sponge, listening to the baseball game on the transistor radio. The Yankees, he loved the Yankees. Mickey Mantle was his absolute favorite. He even bought a baseball card once at an antique shop, paid five whole dollars for it, which was a lot of money back then. He kept it in a little plastic sleeve in his top drawer, right next to his cufflinks...

Oh, I've wandered off again, haven't I? I really am sorry, doctor. My mind just keeps slipping away. But no... no tears come. Just that same cold feeling where the love used to be.

Helen Miller runs them, and she always brings those little powdered sugar cookies from the bakery on Main Street. I'm not really sure, honestly. I suppose I haven't been paying much attention to meals or the clock. I did make a pot of chicken noodle soup the other day. It was the recipe my mother used to make when I was a little girl. She would always put in extra celery, the really leafy parts, because she said that's where all the flavor was. We used to grow celery in our backyard garden, right next to the tomatoes. Those tomatoes were so big and red, you could just slice them up and eat them like an apple with a little dash of salt. Like I was saying before about my daughter holding my hand... I know I love her, but I just can't feel the warmth of it inside me anymore. Nothing feels good or... or warm. \n\nI used to really enjoy my garden. I had these beautiful hydrangeas out front. They were the blue kind, you know, because of the soil acidity. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he always tried to grow them but his always turned out pink. He was so stubborn, he wouldn't listen when I told him to add coffee grounds to the dirt. He used to drink that instant coffee anyway, which I always thought was just dreadful. How can anyone drink that powdery stuff? Real coffee needs to be percolated. My husband, God rest his soul, bought me a beautiful silver percolator for our tenth anniversary. Or was it our fifteenth? It had this little glass knob on top where you could watch the water bubbling up. \n\nBut... no, I don't think I feel much pleasure in anything right now. It's just all sort of blank. Do you think Mr. Henderson is still watering my hydrangeas while I'm here? I really don't know. I wasn't even there, you see. He had just left my house a little while before. I had ironed his shirt for him that morning. It was a very handsome blue button-down, a cotton blend. You have to be so careful with those cotton blends, though. If the iron is even a little bit too hot, it just scorches the fabric right away and leaves that awful shiny mark on the collar. \n\nMy mother used to have one of those heavy cast-iron presses, the kind you actually had to heat up on the wood stove. Can you imagine doing that today? She would spend all of Monday morning just pressing my father's work trousers. He worked at the bank downtown, the one on the corner of Elm and 4th that closed down back in the eighties. They had the most beautiful marble floors in that lobby. They kept them so polished you could practically see your reflection, and you could hear your shoes clicking so loudly when you walked in to make a deposit. They used to give out those little paper lollipops to the children, the ones with the safety loop handle. \n\nBut... oh, what were we talking about? Did I answer your question? I just... I don't know if I could have stopped it. I really don't. Everything from that day is just a blur.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty? I... I don't know if guilt is the right word. I just feel so empty about it all. Like I said, I wasn't there. I was at home, probably cleaning up the kitchen. I always wipe down the counters after breakfast. I use that lemon-scented cleaner, the one in the yellow bottle. My daughter hates the smell of it, she says it reminds her of a hospital, but I've always loved citrus. We had a lemon tree when I was a girl, out in California. My father planted it when they first moved out there. \n\nCalifornia was so different back then. The air smelled like orange blossoms and the ocean. We used to drive down the coast on Sundays in his old Ford. It was this beautiful deep green color, with those big whitewall tires. He kept it so clean, he would wash it every Saturday afternoon with a bucket of soapy water and a big sponge. He'd let me help sometimes, but I usually just ended up getting more water on myself than the car. \n\nBut... what did you ask me? Guilt? I... I'm not sure. I don't really feel much of anything. It's just... it's all so far away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: Sad? I... well, I wouldn't say I feel sad exactly. It's more like I just don't feel much of anything. It's like being wrapped in a thick wool blanket, you know? The kind that's so heavy you can barely move under it. \n\nI actually have a blanket just like that at home. It's a Pendleton, a beautiful plaid pattern with deep reds and forest greens. My husband and I bought it on a trip to Oregon, oh, it must have been forty years ago now. We drove all the way up the coast from California. The Redwoods were just magnificent. We stayed in this little cabin near Eureka, and the fog would roll in so thick off the ocean every morning you couldn't see the car parked right outside the window. We had breakfast at this tiny diner where the waitress called everyone \"hon\" and they served the biggest blueberry pancakes I've ever seen. The syrup came in these little glass pitchers with a metal thumb-flap on the lid. I always try to find those pitchers for my own kitchen, but the new ones just feel so flimsy, don't they? Everything is made of plastic now. \n\nBut... oh dear, I'm sorry. Sadness. No, I don't think I'm sad. I just... I don't feel much at all. Just that heavy, blank feeling.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt this emptiness and lack of feeling inside?\n\nPatient: How long? Well, let me think... it's been exactly six days. Ever since he was killed right outside the community center. \n\nYou know, they just painted the front doors of that community center a bright cherry red last spring. I always thought it was a bit loud for such an old brick building, but the mayor's wife insisted on it. She was in charge of the local beautification committee. I used to be on a committee just like that, back when we lived in Pasadena in the sixties. We helped organize one of the smaller floats for the Rose Parade. We used thousands of real marigolds one year, and the smell was just so strong, very earthy and sharp. You had to glue them on one by one by one. My fingers were sticky with floral glue for a week, and I somehow got it all over my wedding ring. My husband had to take it to the jeweler on Colorado Boulevard to have it professionally cleaned. The jeweler was this lovely older gentleman who always wore a silk bow tie, even in the dead of summer... \n\nOh dear, I'm rambling again, aren't I? I'm so sorry. My mind just wanders off. What did you ask me? How many days? Yes, six days. It's just been six days of this... this nothingness.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy before your grandson's death that you still find some pleasure in now?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? I... no. No, I don't think so. I actually came in today because... well, my daughter was here earlier. She can sit right beside me, holding my hand, and I know in my head that I love her, but I just... I cannot feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It's just flat. \n\nShe has such beautiful hands, my daughter. Long, elegant fingers, just like her grandmother. My mother used to play the piano, you know. A beautiful upright Baldwin we had in the parlor. She would play Chopin on Sunday afternoons while my father read the paper. The Sunday paper was enormous back then, it seemed to take up the whole dining room table. He always read the business section first, and he'd smoke his pipe. It was a cherrywood pipe, and the tobacco smelled so sweet, like vanilla and roasted nuts. He kept it in a special humidor on his desk, a heavy brass box with a little dial on the front. I used to love spinning that dial when he wasn't looking. The desk was mahogany, very solid, with clawed feet that looked like eagles holding glass balls... \n\nI'm sorry, I'm doing it again. You asked about things I enjoy. No, I don't feel pleasure from anything right now. Even when I should, like with my daughter... there's just no feeling there.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that still bring you a little bit of joy or happiness?\n\nPatient: Joy? No, doctor. I... I really don't think so. I know I just said this, but it's really why I'm here. It's my daughter. She was just here, sitting right next to me, holding my hand so tightly. And I look at her, and my brain tells me, \"This is your daughter, you love her,\" but... there's just no warmth. None at all. It's like the feeling is broken. \n\nShe was wearing a lovely yellow sweater today, though. A very soft cashmere. I bought her one just like it when she went off to college, oh, it must have been 1978. Or was it '79? She went to school in Boston, and the winters there are just dreadful. I remember visiting her in February once, and the snow was piled up higher than the cars on the street. We tried to walk to this little Italian bakery she liked in the North End, but the wind was blowing right off the harbor, cutting right through my coat. My coat was a heavy wool pea coat, navy blue with those big anchor buttons. You don't see buttons like that anymore, they were real brass, heavy and cold to the touch. The bakery smelled like anise and toasted almonds, and they had these little cookies shaped like pine nuts...\n\nBut no, I'm sorry. Joy. No, I don't feel any joy or happiness, not even with my own daughter holding my hand. Just... nothing.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or things that you think should bring you happiness, but don't?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, that's exactly it. That's why I'm sitting here talking to you. It's like I've been trying to explain... my daughter, she was just here. She was holding my hand, and I know I love her. I should be feeling that love, that happiness of having her close by, especially now, after... after my grandson was killed. But there's just... I cannot feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It's completely gone.\n\nWe used to hold hands all the time when she was a little girl. We'd walk down to the corner store—Mr. Hooper's store, it was called. He always had those big glass jars of penny candy lined up on the counter. The root beer barrels were her favorite, but I always preferred the little strawberry candies with the soft centers. They came in wrappers that looked like real strawberries. You had to be careful, though, because if they sat in the jar too long during the summer, they'd all melt together into one giant, sticky lump. Mr. Hooper had this big ceiling fan that just slowly spun around, tick-tick-tick, trying to keep the place cool, but it never really worked. He had a calico cat that slept on top of the ice cream freezer... \n\nOh, goodness, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I'm so sorry, doctor. My mind is just a sieve lately. But yes, to answer your question... things that should make me happy, like my daughter being here... I just can't feel it. There's no warmth at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have moments where you feel something, anything, even if it's just for a brief instant, since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I know I must sound like a broken record to you, doctor, I really do. I feel like I'm just saying the exact same thing over and over. But no, not even for a second. That is the entire reason I came in today. Ever since my grandson was killed... my daughter can sit right beside me, just like she was doing earlier, holding my hand, and I know I love her, but I just cannot feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It's just empty. \n\nWhen she was holding my hand today, I was looking at her wedding band. It's a very simple gold band, very traditional. My own wedding band is gold, too, 18 karat. We bought them at a little jeweler downtown, Mr. Peterson's shop. He had this little jeweler's loupe, but he also had a real monocle he wore sometimes. You never see people wearing monocles anymore, do you? It was attached to a fine black ribbon. He had a pocket watch, too, on a long gold chain that draped across his waistcoat. His vest was a dark green velvet, with tiny little pearl buttons all down the front. The shop always smelled like brass polish and old paper, and he kept mints in a little crystal dish by the register... \n\nBut no. To answer you, no. Not even for a brief instant. Just... nothing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are to blame in any way for your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Blame? No, no, I don't think so. I mean, how could I be? I wasn't even there. I was at home, sitting in my favorite armchair. It's a floral print, very comfortable, with those rolled arms. I bought it at a furniture store that used to be on Main Street... oh, what was it called? Sullivan's. Yes, Sullivan's Furniture. Mr. Sullivan was a very tall man, always wore a bowtie. He had this booming voice that would just echo through the showroom. The showroom had these huge plate glass windows, and in the winter, the condensation would freeze on the inside, making these beautiful, intricate frost patterns. They looked like little ferns and stars, delicate little ice crystals. I used to trace them with my finger when I was a girl, waiting for my mother to finish looking at the dining sets. She loved mahogany, always said it was the only wood fit for a proper dining room... \n\nBut, oh, I'm sorry. No, I don't feel like I'm to blame. I just... I don't feel much of anything at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, I... I don't think so. I mean, like I said, I wasn't there when it happened, so there wasn't anything I could have done differently. I was just at home. I was actually watching that old game show on television, the one with the spinning wheel. Pat Sajak, I think his name is? Though I always preferred the older hosts, from when television was still in black and white. We got our first television set in 1956. It was a Zenith, a big heavy wooden cabinet model. My husband, rest his soul, he had to get the neighbor, Mr. Henderson, to help him carry it up the front steps. Mr. Henderson was a stout man, always smoked a pipe. Cherry tobacco, it smelled like. He worked down at the railyard, and he'd come home with soot on his collar. His wife, Martha, she was famous in the neighborhood for her peach cobbler. She'd bring one over every time somebody moved in or had a baby. She used fresh peaches from a tree in her own backyard... \n\nOh, dear, I've lost the thread again, haven't I? I'm so sorry. But no, to answer you... I don't feel guilty. I just feel so... empty.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have moments where you feel like your emotional numbness lifts, even if it's just for a very short time?\n\nPatient: I know we just talked about this, doctor, I really do feel like I'm repeating myself. But no, it never lifts. Like I told you earlier, that's exactly why I made this appointment in the first place. Ever since my grandson was killed, my daughter can sit right beside me, holding my hand, and I know I love her, I really do, but I just cannot feel the warmth of it inside me at all. Nothing. \n\nHer hands were a bit chilly today, actually. She's always had poor circulation, just like her father. My husband used to wear these thick wool socks around the house, even in the dead of summer. Gray ones, with a little red stripe at the top. We used to order them from a catalog that came in the mail. Our mail carrier back then was a lovely man named Arthur. He had a terrible limp, poor thing, from the war, but he never complained. He carried this massive leather satchel, real thick cowhide. It had a brass buckle that was always polished so bright you could see your reflection in it. I had a handbag with a buckle like that once, bought it on a trip to Chicago in... oh, it must have been 1968. We took the train, the Pullman cars, and the dining car had these pristine white tablecloths and heavy silver cutlery. They don't make silverware like that anymore, it all feels so flimsy now... \n\nOh, dear. I'm doing it again, aren't I? I am so sorry. But no, to answer your question, it never lifts. It's just that same emptiness all the time.\n\nDoctor: Has the emptiness you've been feeling changed in intensity at all since it started six days ago?\n\nPatient: Changed? No, I don't... I don't think so. It's been exactly the same since Tuesday. Six days now, just... just this flat, hollow feeling. Tuesday was always grocery day for me and my sister, Eleanor. We'd go to the A&P down on 4th Street. They had the best produce section, always kept the apples so shiny. Eleanor loved the Granny Smiths, said they were the only ones tart enough for a proper pie. She baked a pie for the church bake sale every single year, right up until her arthritis got too bad. Her hands would just swell up terribly, poor thing. She tried wearing those copper bracelets, you know, the ones they used to advertise in the back of magazines? I never believed they worked, but she swore by them. She ordered hers from a catalog, I think it was... oh, maybe Sears Roebuck? They used to sell everything in those catalogs, even entire houses. Kit houses, they called them. You'd order it, and all the lumber and nails would just arrive on a train... \n\nI'm sorry, doctor. I'm drifting again. But no, the emptiness hasn't changed at all. It's just constant.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're about to cry or show some emotion, but it never actually happens?\n\nPatient: Cry? Oh, I... I don't know. Maybe I feel like I should be crying, but nothing happens. Like I was telling you before, it's just this awful blankness. That's really why I'm sitting here talking to you today. Ever since my grandson passed, my daughter will come over and just hold my hand, and I know in my head that I love her so much, but I just can't feel any of that warmth inside. It's just... nothing. \n\nShe held my hand just like that when she was a little girl, crossing the street. We lived on Elm Street then, and the traffic could be so dreadful, especially when the factory let out at 5 o'clock. The whistle would blow, you could hear it all over town, and then all those cars would just come pouring down the hill. Mostly Fords and Chevrolets back then, big heavy things with those beautiful chrome bumpers. My husband drove a Bel Air, a two-tone blue and white. He kept it so clean you could eat off the hood. Every Sunday afternoon, he'd be out there with a bucket and a sponge, listening to the baseball game on the transistor radio. The Yankees, he loved the Yankees. Mickey Mantle was his absolute favorite. He even bought a baseball card once at an antique shop, paid five whole dollars for it, which was a lot of money back then. He kept it in a little plastic sleeve in his top drawer, right next to his cufflinks...\n\nOh, I've wandered off again, haven't I? I really am sorry, doctor. My mind just keeps slipping away. But no... no tears come. Just that same cold feeling where the love used to be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I feel like I'm just saying the exact same things over and over to you, doctor. I know you're trying to understand, but... no, I'm never on the verge of feeling it. Like I've been trying to explain, it's the whole reason I made this appointment. Ever since my grandson was killed, my daughter sits right beside me and holds my hand, and my brain knows I love her, but that warmth... that feeling of love? It just isn't there anymore. Not even a spark of it. 

She has such lovely hands, though. She plays the piano, you know. Started when she was just six years old. We bought her this upright Baldwin piano for her birthday. It was second-hand, but it had a beautiful, rich tone. The man who tuned it for us was blind, Mr. Henderson. He had the most incredible ear, could hear a pin drop from three rooms away. He used to take the city bus all over town with his guide dog, a golden retriever named Barnaby. Oh, Barnaby was such a sweet dog. He loved having his ears scratched, right behind the floppy part. My neighbor Mrs. Gable had a dog just like him, used to dig up all my prize hydrangeas... she tried putting chicken wire down, but he'd just dig right under it. You really had to bury it deep to keep the animals out of the garden back then...

Oh, listen to me. I'm so sorry, my mind is just a complete sieve today. But no, to answer you... I don't feel like I'm on the verge of anything. It's just completely gone.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.21, F32.0, F33.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I... I came in because... ever since my grandson was killed... things just haven't been right. My daughter, she's been coming over, and she'll sit right beside me on the sofa and just hold my hand. She has such delicate hands, you know. She used to play the piano so beautifully when she was a girl. We had this lovely mahogany upright—or was it oak? I think it was oak. I always told her she could have been a concert pianist if she’d just kept at it instead of going off to study business at the university. But, oh, what was I saying? \n\nRight, so... she sits there holding my hand, and I know I love her. I know I do. But... I cannot feel the warmth of it inside me at all. Not anymore. It's just... nothing. I don't know why. \n\nDid I mention I might have left my porch light on? It looked like rain when she drove me here, and I hate coming back to a dark house...\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice you couldn't feel the warmth of your daughter's hand?\n\nPatient: Let me think... it's been exactly six days. Yes, six days since... since my grandson was killed. It happened right outside the community center, you know. \n\nI used to go to that very community center every Wednesday morning for the bingo games. Helen Miller runs them, and she always brings those little powdered sugar cookies from the bakery on Main Street. I asked her to pick some up for me once, but she said they only sell them by the dozen and she didn't want to carry the large box. Can you imagine? It’s just a cardboard box. But... yes, it was right outside those front doors where it happened. Six days ago. \n\nAnd ever since that very day, I just... I look at my daughter's hand on mine, and I feel absolutely nothing. She had her nails painted a lovely shade of peach recently, though. The salon she goes to does such a nice job with cuticles. I should really ask her for their number, though I don't get out much these days. Do you think peach is too young a color for someone my age?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Oh, well... I'm not really sure, honestly. I suppose I haven't been paying much attention to meals or the clock. I did make a pot of chicken noodle soup the other day. It was the recipe my mother used to make when I was a little girl. She would always put in extra celery, the really leafy parts, because she said that's where all the flavor was. We used to grow celery in our backyard garden, right next to the tomatoes. Those tomatoes were so big and red, you could just slice them up and eat them like an apple with a little dash of salt. Have you ever tried that? It's wonderful in the summer. \n\nBut... what were you asking? Oh, appetite. I don't really know if it's changed. I just... I haven't really noticed. I think I left the soup on the stove too long anyway, the noodles got all mushy. I hate mushy noodles, don't you?\n\nDoctor: Do you find pleasure in things you used to enjoy before your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? Oh... I don't know. Like I was saying before about my daughter holding my hand... I know I love her, but I just can't feel the warmth of it inside me anymore. Nothing feels good or... or warm. \n\nI used to really enjoy my garden. I had these beautiful hydrangeas out front. They were the blue kind, you know, because of the soil acidity. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he always tried to grow them but his always turned out pink. He was so stubborn, he wouldn't listen when I told him to add coffee grounds to the dirt. He used to drink that instant coffee anyway, which I always thought was just dreadful. How can anyone drink that powdery stuff? Real coffee needs to be percolated. My husband, God rest his soul, bought me a beautiful silver percolator for our tenth anniversary. Or was it our fifteenth? It had this little glass knob on top where you could watch the water bubbling up. \n\nBut... no, I don't think I feel much pleasure in anything right now. It's just all sort of blank. Do you think Mr. Henderson is still watering my hydrangeas while I'm here? I left him a spare key, but he's getting so forgetful these days.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there's anything you could have done to prevent your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Prevent it? I... I really don't know. I wasn't even there, you see. He had just left my house a little while before. I had ironed his shirt for him that morning. It was a very handsome blue button-down, a cotton blend. You have to be so careful with those cotton blends, though. If the iron is even a little bit too hot, it just scorches the fabric right away and leaves that awful shiny mark on the collar. \n\nMy mother used to have one of those heavy cast-iron presses, the kind you actually had to heat up on the wood stove. Can you imagine doing that today? She would spend all of Monday morning just pressing my father's work trousers. He worked at the bank downtown, the one on the corner of Elm and 4th that closed down back in the eighties. They had the most beautiful marble floors in that lobby. They kept them so polished you could practically see your reflection, and you could hear your shoes clicking so loudly when you walked in to make a deposit. They used to give out those little paper lollipops to the children, the ones with the safety loop handle. \n\nBut... oh, what were we talking about? Did I answer your question? I just... I don't know if I could have stopped it. I really don't. Everything from that day is just a blur.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty? I... I don't know if guilt is the right word. I just feel so empty about it all. Like I said, I wasn't there. I was at home, probably cleaning up the kitchen. I always wipe down the counters after breakfast. I use that lemon-scented cleaner, the one in the yellow bottle. My daughter hates the smell of it, she says it reminds her of a hospital, but I've always loved citrus. We had a lemon tree when I was a girl, out in California. My father planted it when they first moved out there. \n\nCalifornia was so different back then. The air smelled like orange blossoms and the ocean. We used to drive down the coast on Sundays in his old Ford. It was this beautiful deep green color, with those big whitewall tires. He kept it so clean, he would wash it every Saturday afternoon with a bucket of soapy water and a big sponge. He'd let me help sometimes, but I usually just ended up getting more water on myself than the car. \n\nBut... what did you ask me? Guilt? I... I'm not sure. I don't really feel much of anything. It's just... it's all so far away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: Sad? I... well, I wouldn't say I feel sad exactly. It's more like I just don't feel much of anything. It's like being wrapped in a thick wool blanket, you know? The kind that's so heavy you can barely move under it. \n\nI actually have a blanket just like that at home. It's a Pendleton, a beautiful plaid pattern with deep reds and forest greens. My husband and I bought it on a trip to Oregon, oh, it must have been forty years ago now. We drove all the way up the coast from California. The Redwoods were just magnificent. We stayed in this little cabin near Eureka, and the fog would roll in so thick off the ocean every morning you couldn't see the car parked right outside the window. We had breakfast at this tiny diner where the waitress called everyone \"hon\" and they served the biggest blueberry pancakes I've ever seen. The syrup came in these little glass pitchers with a metal thumb-flap on the lid. I always try to find those pitchers for my own kitchen, but the new ones just feel so flimsy, don't they? Everything is made of plastic now. \n\nBut... oh dear, I'm sorry. Sadness. No, I don't think I'm sad. I just... I don't feel much at all. Just that heavy, blank feeling.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt this emptiness and lack of feeling inside?\n\nPatient: How long? Well, let me think... it's been exactly six days. Ever since he was killed right outside the community center. \n\nYou know, they just painted the front doors of that community center a bright cherry red last spring. I always thought it was a bit loud for such an old brick building, but the mayor's wife insisted on it. She was in charge of the local beautification committee. I used to be on a committee just like that, back when we lived in Pasadena in the sixties. We helped organize one of the smaller floats for the Rose Parade. We used thousands of real marigolds one year, and the smell was just so strong, very earthy and sharp. You had to glue them on one by one by one. My fingers were sticky with floral glue for a week, and I somehow got it all over my wedding ring. My husband had to take it to the jeweler on Colorado Boulevard to have it professionally cleaned. The jeweler was this lovely older gentleman who always wore a silk bow tie, even in the dead of summer... \n\nOh dear, I'm rambling again, aren't I? I'm so sorry. My mind just wanders off. What did you ask me? How many days? Yes, six days. It's just been six days of this... this nothingness.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy before your grandson's death that you still find some pleasure in now?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? I... no. No, I don't think so. I actually came in today because... well, my daughter was here earlier. She can sit right beside me, holding my hand, and I know in my head that I love her, but I just... I cannot feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It's just flat. \n\nShe has such beautiful hands, my daughter. Long, elegant fingers, just like her grandmother. My mother used to play the piano, you know. A beautiful upright Baldwin we had in the parlor. She would play Chopin on Sunday afternoons while my father read the paper. The Sunday paper was enormous back then, it seemed to take up the whole dining room table. He always read the business section first, and he'd smoke his pipe. It was a cherrywood pipe, and the tobacco smelled so sweet, like vanilla and roasted nuts. He kept it in a special humidor on his desk, a heavy brass box with a little dial on the front. I used to love spinning that dial when he wasn't looking. The desk was mahogany, very solid, with clawed feet that looked like eagles holding glass balls... \n\nI'm sorry, I'm doing it again. You asked about things I enjoy. No, I don't feel pleasure from anything right now. Even when I should, like with my daughter... there's just no feeling there.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that still bring you a little bit of joy or happiness?\n\nPatient: Joy? No, doctor. I... I really don't think so. I know I just said this, but it's really why I'm here. It's my daughter. She was just here, sitting right next to me, holding my hand so tightly. And I look at her, and my brain tells me, \"This is your daughter, you love her,\" but... there's just no warmth. None at all. It's like the feeling is broken. \n\nShe was wearing a lovely yellow sweater today, though. A very soft cashmere. I bought her one just like it when she went off to college, oh, it must have been 1978. Or was it '79? She went to school in Boston, and the winters there are just dreadful. I remember visiting her in February once, and the snow was piled up higher than the cars on the street. We tried to walk to this little Italian bakery she liked in the North End, but the wind was blowing right off the harbor, cutting right through my coat. My coat was a heavy wool pea coat, navy blue with those big anchor buttons. You don't see buttons like that anymore, they were real brass, heavy and cold to the touch. The bakery smelled like anise and toasted almonds, and they had these little cookies shaped like pine nuts...\n\nBut no, I'm sorry. Joy. No, I don't feel any joy or happiness, not even with my own daughter holding my hand. Just... nothing.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or things that you think should bring you happiness, but don't?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, that's exactly it. That's why I'm sitting here talking to you. It's like I've been trying to explain... my daughter, she was just here. She was holding my hand, and I know I love her. I should be feeling that love, that happiness of having her close by, especially now, after... after my grandson was killed. But there's just... I cannot feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It's completely gone.\n\nWe used to hold hands all the time when she was a little girl. We'd walk down to the corner store—Mr. Hooper's store, it was called. He always had those big glass jars of penny candy lined up on the counter. The root beer barrels were her favorite, but I always preferred the little strawberry candies with the soft centers. They came in wrappers that looked like real strawberries. You had to be careful, though, because if they sat in the jar too long during the summer, they'd all melt together into one giant, sticky lump. Mr. Hooper had this big ceiling fan that just slowly spun around, tick-tick-tick, trying to keep the place cool, but it never really worked. He had a calico cat that slept on top of the ice cream freezer... \n\nOh, goodness, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I'm so sorry, doctor. My mind is just a sieve lately. But yes, to answer your question... things that should make me happy, like my daughter being here... I just can't feel it. There's no warmth at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have moments where you feel something, anything, even if it's just for a brief instant, since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I know I must sound like a broken record to you, doctor, I really do. I feel like I'm just saying the exact same thing over and over. But no, not even for a second. That is the entire reason I came in today. Ever since my grandson was killed... my daughter can sit right beside me, just like she was doing earlier, holding my hand, and I know I love her, but I just cannot feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It's just empty. \n\nWhen she was holding my hand today, I was looking at her wedding band. It's a very simple gold band, very traditional. My own wedding band is gold, too, 18 karat. We bought them at a little jeweler downtown, Mr. Peterson's shop. He had this little jeweler's loupe, but he also had a real monocle he wore sometimes. You never see people wearing monocles anymore, do you? It was attached to a fine black ribbon. He had a pocket watch, too, on a long gold chain that draped across his waistcoat. His vest was a dark green velvet, with tiny little pearl buttons all down the front. The shop always smelled like brass polish and old paper, and he kept mints in a little crystal dish by the register... \n\nBut no. To answer you, no. Not even for a brief instant. Just... nothing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are to blame in any way for your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Blame? No, no, I don't think so. I mean, how could I be? I wasn't even there. I was at home, sitting in my favorite armchair. It's a floral print, very comfortable, with those rolled arms. I bought it at a furniture store that used to be on Main Street... oh, what was it called? Sullivan's. Yes, Sullivan's Furniture. Mr. Sullivan was a very tall man, always wore a bowtie. He had this booming voice that would just echo through the showroom. The showroom had these huge plate glass windows, and in the winter, the condensation would freeze on the inside, making these beautiful, intricate frost patterns. They looked like little ferns and stars, delicate little ice crystals. I used to trace them with my finger when I was a girl, waiting for my mother to finish looking at the dining sets. She loved mahogany, always said it was the only wood fit for a proper dining room... \n\nBut, oh, I'm sorry. No, I don't feel like I'm to blame. I just... I don't feel much of anything at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, I... I don't think so. I mean, like I said, I wasn't there when it happened, so there wasn't anything I could have done differently. I was just at home. I was actually watching that old game show on television, the one with the spinning wheel. Pat Sajak, I think his name is? Though I always preferred the older hosts, from when television was still in black and white. We got our first television set in 1956. It was a Zenith, a big heavy wooden cabinet model. My husband, rest his soul, he had to get the neighbor, Mr. Henderson, to help him carry it up the front steps. Mr. Henderson was a stout man, always smoked a pipe. Cherry tobacco, it smelled like. He worked down at the railyard, and he'd come home with soot on his collar. His wife, Martha, she was famous in the neighborhood for her peach cobbler. She'd bring one over every time somebody moved in or had a baby. She used fresh peaches from a tree in her own backyard... \n\nOh, dear, I've lost the thread again, haven't I? I'm so sorry. But no, to answer you... I don't feel guilty. I just feel so... empty.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have moments where you feel like your emotional numbness lifts, even if it's just for a very short time?\n\nPatient: I know we just talked about this, doctor, I really do feel like I'm repeating myself. But no, it never lifts. Like I told you earlier, that's exactly why I made this appointment in the first place. Ever since my grandson was killed, my daughter can sit right beside me, holding my hand, and I know I love her, I really do, but I just cannot feel the warmth of it inside me at all. Nothing. \n\nHer hands were a bit chilly today, actually. She's always had poor circulation, just like her father. My husband used to wear these thick wool socks around the house, even in the dead of summer. Gray ones, with a little red stripe at the top. We used to order them from a catalog that came in the mail. Our mail carrier back then was a lovely man named Arthur. He had a terrible limp, poor thing, from the war, but he never complained. He carried this massive leather satchel, real thick cowhide. It had a brass buckle that was always polished so bright you could see your reflection in it. I had a handbag with a buckle like that once, bought it on a trip to Chicago in... oh, it must have been 1968. We took the train, the Pullman cars, and the dining car had these pristine white tablecloths and heavy silver cutlery. They don't make silverware like that anymore, it all feels so flimsy now... \n\nOh, dear. I'm doing it again, aren't I? I am so sorry. But no, to answer your question, it never lifts. It's just that same emptiness all the time.\n\nDoctor: Has the emptiness you've been feeling changed in intensity at all since it started six days ago?\n\nPatient: Changed? No, I don't... I don't think so. It's been exactly the same since Tuesday. Six days now, just... just this flat, hollow feeling. Tuesday was always grocery day for me and my sister, Eleanor. We'd go to the A&P down on 4th Street. They had the best produce section, always kept the apples so shiny. Eleanor loved the Granny Smiths, said they were the only ones tart enough for a proper pie. She baked a pie for the church bake sale every single year, right up until her arthritis got too bad. Her hands would just swell up terribly, poor thing. She tried wearing those copper bracelets, you know, the ones they used to advertise in the back of magazines? I never believed they worked, but she swore by them. She ordered hers from a catalog, I think it was... oh, maybe Sears Roebuck? They used to sell everything in those catalogs, even entire houses. Kit houses, they called them. You'd order it, and all the lumber and nails would just arrive on a train... \n\nI'm sorry, doctor. I'm drifting again. But no, the emptiness hasn't changed at all. It's just constant.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're about to cry or show some emotion, but it never actually happens?\n\nPatient: Cry? Oh, I... I don't know. Maybe I feel like I should be crying, but nothing happens. Like I was telling you before, it's just this awful blankness. That's really why I'm sitting here talking to you today. Ever since my grandson passed, my daughter will come over and just hold my hand, and I know in my head that I love her so much, but I just can't feel any of that warmth inside. It's just... nothing. \n\nShe held my hand just like that when she was a little girl, crossing the street. We lived on Elm Street then, and the traffic could be so dreadful, especially when the factory let out at 5 o'clock. The whistle would blow, you could hear it all over town, and then all those cars would just come pouring down the hill. Mostly Fords and Chevrolets back then, big heavy things with those beautiful chrome bumpers. My husband drove a Bel Air, a two-tone blue and white. He kept it so clean you could eat off the hood. Every Sunday afternoon, he'd be out there with a bucket and a sponge, listening to the baseball game on the transistor radio. The Yankees, he loved the Yankees. Mickey Mantle was his absolute favorite. He even bought a baseball card once at an antique shop, paid five whole dollars for it, which was a lot of money back then. He kept it in a little plastic sleeve in his top drawer, right next to his cufflinks...\n\nOh, I've wandered off again, haven't I? I really am sorry, doctor. My mind just keeps slipping away. But no... no tears come. Just that same cold feeling where the love used to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're on the verge of feeling something, but it never actually happens?\n\nPatient: I feel like I'm just saying the exact same things over and over to you, doctor. I know you're trying to understand, but... no, I'm never on the verge of feeling it. Like I've been trying to explain, it's the whole reason I made this appointment. Ever since my grandson was killed, my daughter sits right beside me and holds my hand, and my brain knows I love her, but that warmth... that feeling of love? It just isn't there anymore. Not even a spark of it. \n\nShe has such lovely hands, though. She plays the piano, you know. Started when she was just six years old. We bought her this upright Baldwin piano for her birthday. It was second-hand, but it had a beautiful, rich tone. The man who tuned it for us was blind, Mr. Henderson. He had the most incredible ear, could hear a pin drop from three rooms away. He used to take the city bus all over town with his guide dog, a golden retriever named Barnaby. Oh, Barnaby was such a sweet dog. He loved having his ears scratched, right behind the floppy part. My neighbor Mrs. Gable had a dog just like him, used to dig up all my prize hydrangeas... she tried putting chicken wire down, but he'd just dig right under it. You really had to bury it deep to keep the animals out of the garden back then...\n\nOh, listen to me. I'm so sorry, my mind is just a complete sieve today. But no, to answer you... I don't feel like I'm on the verge of anything. It's just completely gone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. The shop always smelled like brass polish and old paper, and he kept mints in a little crystal dish by the register... \n\nBut no. To answer you, no. Not even for a brief instant. Just... nothing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are to blame in any way for your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Blame? No, no, I don't think so. I mean, how could I be? I wasn't even there. I was at home, sitting in my favorite armchair. It's a floral print, very comfortable, with those rolled arms. I bought it at a furniture store that used to be on Main Street... oh, what was it called? Sullivan's. Yes, Sullivan's Furniture. Mr. Sullivan was a very tall man, always wore a bowtie. He had this booming voice that would just echo through the showroom. The showroom had these huge plate glass windows, and in the winter, the condensation would freeze on the inside, making these beautiful, intricate frost patterns. They looked like little ferns and stars, delicate little ice crystals. I used to trace them with my finger when I was a girl, waiting for my mother to finish looking at the dining sets. She loved mahogany, always said it was the only wood fit for a proper dining room... \n\nBut, oh, I'm sorry. No, I don't feel like I'm to blame. I just... I don't feel much of anything at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to your grandson's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, I... I don't think so. I mean, like I said, I wasn't there when it happened, so there wasn't anything I could have done differently. I was just at home. I was actually watching that old game show on television, the one with the spinning wheel. Pat Sajak, I think his name is? Though I always preferred the older hosts, from when television was still in black and white. We got our first television set in 1956. It was a Zenith, a big heavy wooden cabinet model. My husband, rest his soul, he had to get the neighbor, Mr. Henderson, to help him carry it up the front steps. Mr. Henderson was a stout man, always smoked a pipe. Cherry tobacco, it smelled like. He worked down at the railyard, and he'd come home with soot on his collar. His wife, Martha, she was famous in the neighborhood for her peach cobbler. She'd bring one over every time somebody moved in or had a baby. She used fresh peaches from a tree in her own backyard... \n\nOh, dear, I've lost the thread again, haven't I? I'm so sorry. But no, to answer you... I don't feel guilty. I just feel so... empty.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have moments where you feel like your emotional numbness lifts, even if it's just for a very short time?\n\nPatient: I know we just talked about this, doctor, I really do feel like I'm repeating myself. But no, it never lifts. Like I told you earlier, that's exactly why I made this appointment in the first place. Ever since my grandson was killed, my daughter can sit right beside me, holding my hand, and I know I love her, I really do, but I just cannot feel the warmth of it inside me at all. Nothing. \n\nHer hands were a bit chilly today, actually. She's always had poor circulation, just like her father. My husband used to wear these thick wool socks around the house, even in the dead of summer. Gray ones, with a little red stripe at the top. We used to order them from a catalog that came in the mail. Our mail carrier back then was a lovely man named Arthur. He had a terrible limp, poor thing, from the war, but he never complained. He carried this massive leather satchel, real thick cowhide. It had a brass buckle that was always polished so bright you could see your reflection in it. I had a handbag with a buckle like that once, bought it on a trip to Chicago in... oh, it must have been 1968. We took the train, the Pullman cars, and the dining car had these pristine white tablecloths and heavy silver cutlery. They don't make silverware like that anymore, it all feels so flimsy now... \n\nOh, dear. I'm doing it again, aren't I? I am so sorry. But no, to answer your question, it never lifts. It's just that same emptiness all the time.\n\nDoctor: Has the emptiness you've been feeling changed in intensity at all since it started six days ago?\n\nPatient: Changed? No, I don't... I don't think so. It's been exactly the same since Tuesday. Six days now, just... just this flat, hollow feeling. Tuesday was always grocery day for me and my sister, Eleanor. We'd go to the A&P down on 4th Street. They had the best produce section, always kept the apples so shiny. Eleanor loved the Granny Smiths, said they were the only ones tart enough for a proper pie. She baked a pie for the church bake sale every single year, right up until her arthritis got too bad. Her hands would just swell up terribly, poor thing. She tried wearing those copper bracelets, you know, the ones they used to advertise in the back of magazines? I never believed they worked, but she swore by them. She ordered hers from a catalog, I think it was... oh, maybe Sears Roebuck? They used to sell everything in those catalogs, even entire houses. Kit houses, they called them. You'd order it, and all the lumber and nails would just arrive on a train... \n\nI'm sorry, doctor. I'm drifting again. But no, the emptiness hasn't changed at all. It's just constant.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're about to cry or show some emotion, but it never actually happens?\n\nPatient: Cry? Oh, I... I don't know. Maybe I feel like I should be crying, but nothing happens. Like I was telling you before, it's just this awful blankness. That's really why I'm sitting here talking to you today. Ever since my grandson passed, my daughter will come over and just hold my hand, and I know in my head that I love her so much, but I just can't feel any of that warmth inside. It's just... nothing. \n\nShe held my hand just like that when she was a little girl, crossing the street. We lived on Elm Street then, and the traffic could be so dreadful, especially when the factory let out at 5 o'clock. The whistle would blow, you could hear it all over town, and then all those cars would just come pouring down the hill. Mostly Fords and Chevrolets back then, big heavy things with those beautiful chrome bumpers. My husband drove a Bel Air, a two-tone blue and white. He kept it so clean you could eat off the hood. Every Sunday afternoon, he'd be out there with a bucket and a sponge, listening to the baseball game on the transistor radio. The Yankees, he loved the Yankees. Mickey Mantle was his absolute favorite. He even bought a baseball card once at an antique shop, paid five whole dollars for it, which was a lot of money back then. He kept it in a little plastic sleeve in his top drawer, right next to his cufflinks...\n\nOh, I've wandered off again, haven't I? I really am sorry, doctor. My mind just keeps slipping away. But no... no tears come. Just that same cold feeling where the love used to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're on the verge of feeling something, but it never actually happens?\n\nPatient: I feel like I'm just saying the exact same things over and over to you, doctor. I know you're trying to understand, but... no, I'm never on the verge of feeling it. Like I've been trying to explain, it's the whole reason I made this appointment. Ever since my grandson was killed, my daughter sits right beside me and holds my hand, and my brain knows I love her, but that warmth... that feeling of love? It just isn't there anymore. Not even a spark of it. \n\nShe has such lovely hands, though. She plays the piano, you know. Started when she was just six years old. We bought her this upright Baldwin piano for her birthday. It was second-hand, but it had a beautiful, rich tone. The man who tuned it for us was blind, Mr. Henderson. He had the most incredible ear, could hear a pin drop from three rooms away. He used to take the city bus all over town with his guide dog, a golden retriever named Barnaby. Oh, Barnaby was such a sweet dog. He loved having his ears scratched, right behind the floppy part. My neighbor Mrs. Gable had a dog just like him, used to dig up all my prize hydrangeas... she tried putting chicken wire down, but he'd just dig right under it. You really had to bury it deep to keep the animals out of the garden back then...\n\nOh, listen to me. I'm so sorry, my mind is just a complete sieve today. But no, to answer you... I don't feel like I'm on the verge of anything. It's just completely gone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I... I feel like a broken record, doctor. I know you're asking about breaking down, or almost showing something, but... no. It doesn't pass, because it never even starts. Like I've been trying to tell you, it's just this awful, complete blankness. That's why I'm here. Since my grandson was killed... my daughter, my own daughter, sits right there next to me and holds my hand. And in my head, I know I love her. I know it. But I just can't feel that warmth inside me at all. There's no breakdown, no almost... just nothing.

She was wearing a yellow sweater the other day when she came over. It reminded me of this cardigan I knitted for her when she was in high school. It was a mohair blend, very popular back then. I bought the yarn at a little shop downtown, right next to the old movie theater. The Rialto, it was called. We used to go there on Saturday afternoons for the matinees. You could get a ticket, popcorn, and a soda for less than a dollar. They played all the old Hitchcock movies. 'Rear Window' was always my favorite. Grace Kelly was just so elegant in that film, wasn't she? Those dresses she wore, with the full skirts and the pearls... women really dressed up back then, even just to go to the grocery store. You'd never see anyone out in sweatpants like they do today. My mother would have been scandalized. She always wore a hat and gloves, even in the middle of summer. She had this one hat, a wide-brimmed straw thing with a silk ribbon...

Oh, dear. I'm doing it again, aren't I? I'm sorry. But no, I don't feel like I'm about to break down. I don't feel much of anything at all.
